Remark 3

The large combinations of
(N,T)
in our experiments are selected to demonstrate the asymptotic properties of the tests and the effect of weaker loadings. In Section 5.2 of the Online Supplement, we provide a full set of simulations for smaller samples (e.g.,
N=100,T=200,350
) to reflect more practical scenarios. While natural, the loss in power is not substantial. Nevertheless, small sample performance can be improved by bootstrap, because Assumptions 1-5 in
Gonçalves & Perron, (2014)
are analogous to ours. Also, their requirement
\sqrt{T}N^{-1}\to c
for
0\leq c<\infty
is analogous to, for
c=0
, our current Assumption A.5, where
\sqrt{T}N^{-\alpha}\to 0
for
\alpha\in(0.5,1)
. As such, their best-case (i.e., without any asymptotic bias term) bootstrap can be used in our context, too. The bootstrap DGP in this case can be constructed using the wild bootstrap scheme described in Section 4 of their study. Specifically, before Algorithm
1
starts, we generate
\mathbf{X}_{t}^{*}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}\widehat{\mathbf{f}}_{t}+\mathbf{e}_{t}^{*}
and
y_{t+1}^{*}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{z}}_{t}+u_{t+1}^{*}
, where
\widehat{\mathbf{z}}_{t}=(\mathbf{w}_{t}^{\prime},\widehat{\mathbf{f}}_{t}^{\prime})^{\prime}
, while
\widehat{\mathbf{f}}_{t}
,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\delta}}=(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\prime},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\prime})^{\prime}
are the initial estimates obtained using the whole time series sample
t=1,\ldots T
. Next,
\mathbf{e}_{t}^{*}=(\nu_{1,t}\widehat{e}_{1,t},\ldots,\nu_{N,t}\widehat{e}_{N,t})^{\prime}
and
u_{t+1}^{*}=\eta_{t+1}\widehat{u}_{t+1}
, where
\nu_{i,t}
and
\eta_{t}
are IID
(0,1)
mutually independent variables that scale the residuals from the initial regression. Eventually, Algorithm
1
is implemented for
b=1,\ldots,B
bootstrap samples generated according to the wild scheme.

4
Empirical Applications

4.1
Inflation Forecasting

We partially replicate the inflation forecast exercise conducted by
Pitarakis, (2023)
. Our focus here is to explore whether
global
inflation can enhance the accuracy of country-level inflation forecasts. This debate is not new; previous studies have provided evidences that global inflation trends can significantly improve domestic inflation forecasts
(
Monacelli & Sala,, 2009
;
Ciccarelli & Mojon,, 2010
, a.o.,)
. However, other research suggests that the relevance of global inflation in forecasting domestic rates may stem solely from its ability to capture slow-moving trends in inflation
(
Mikolajun & Lodge,, 2016
)
, or that a global inflation factor improves forecasting accuracy primarily at longer horizons
(
Gillitzer & McCarthy,, 2019
)
. The question is, also, how to measure global inflation. One approach is to calculate a grand average of country-level inflation rates, represented as
\bar{\pi}_{t}=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\pi_{i,t}
. Another method involves treating global inflation as a few latent factors that can be estimated using PC from the pool of country-level inflation rates. The former approach is employed by
Pitarakis, (2023)
to illustrate his encompassing test, while we will utilize the latter method to demonstrate how JYP’s encompassing tests work in an empirically relevant context where PC factors are considered. There are several reasons why PC global inflation serves as a more accurate measure than a simple grand average of inflation rates. A straightforward sample mean does not consider differences in economic size, inflation volatility, or other factors that may make some countries’ inflation rates more indicative of global trends than others. In contrast, PCs can uncover patterns of co-movement in inflation rates that might not be evident from the raw data. Additionally, it is more robust to potential outliers and can adapt to time-varying relationships among countries’ inflation rates. However, one could argue that if we assume the existence of only one factor, then all the information contained in that factor is effectively the same as a cross-sectional average of the countries’ inflation rates. This would be true, upon essentially three assumptions: (i) the existence of an
exact
factor model underlying the data, i.e.,
\pi_{i,t}=\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{f}_{t}+e_{i,t}
with
Cov(e_{i,t},e_{j,s})=0
,
t,s\in\mathbb{Z}
,
i,j=1,\ldots,N
,
i\neq j
; (ii) a large cross-sectional dimension
N
(in principle
N\to\infty
); (iii)
all
or
most of
loadings being non-zero (i.e., pervasiveness of factors). Assume that the loadings are fixed. If all these are satisfied, it is clear how
Var(\bar{\pi}_{t})=N^{-2}(\sum_{i}\lambda_{i})^{2}Var(f_{t})+N^{-2}Var(\sum_{i}e_{i,t})\to\bar{\lambda}^{2}=Var(\bar{\chi}_{t}),
as
N\to\infty,
meaning how the aggregation of the observed data recovers the same information contained in the factor. Now, (i) is clearly too strong (see also our A.4, v), (ii) is what is referred to as the “blessing of dimensionality” but in practice it clearly depends on the available data, (iii) is precisely what is challenged by the weaker loadings treated in Section 2. Hence, there are good reasons to re-run this exercise using factors and employing the JYP’s tests to check if global inflation computed by means of PC factors improves the country-level inflation forecast. We employ the same dataset provided in
Pitarakis, (2023)
12
12

12

Freely available on JYP’s GitHub page:
https://github.com/jpitarakis/Multi-Step_Encompassing
, based on the World Bank
global inflation database
covering the period 1970-2023 for 23 countries at quarterly frequency. We choose the tuning parameters in line with the simulations to balance the power and size.
13
13

13

For the exact treatment and transformations of the raw data we refer to
Pitarakis, (2023)
, Section 7.

The base-line model is an
AR(1)
while the alternative model is a factor-augmented
AR(1)
where factors are recursively estimated and their number is determined via
Bai & Ng, (2002)
information criterion (
IC_{p1}
, max number
=10
).
14
14

14

It is well known how the
Bai & Ng, (2002)
criteria depend quite substantially on the maximum number of factors as selected by the practitioner, as well as the relative magnitude of
N,T

(
Forni et al.,, 2009
, see e.g.,)
. In this case, we find on average that all criteria, including
IC_{p1}
but excluding
AIC_{3}
, return the maximum as estimated number of factors. We experimented using
AIC_{3}
and the ABC criterion of
Alessi et al., (2010)
too, which both on average estimate 4/5 common factors, but we found a completely similar picture in terms of significance, with only slightly higher p-values.
We find patterns of significance across the four test statistics, though rarely all of them at once (only AUT).
g_{\widehat{f},4}^{adj}
finds significant better forecast accuracy one quarter ahead when using PC global inflation in
9
of the
23
countries. It is followed by the encompassing test
g_{\widehat{f},1}
with
8
countries (
9
with CAN if considering
10\%
nominal level),
g_{\widehat{f},3}^{adj}
with
7
and
g_{\widehat{f},2}^{adj}
with
4
. Noticeably, for some large economies such as USA, FRA, ESP, at least two test statistics of the four are found significant (JPN too if considering
10\%
nominal level). Some other large/medium-to-large economies like GBR, DEU, ITA have only one or no significance at all. Overall, this paints a mixed picture with regard to the use of global inflation to better forecast country-level inflation, which seems to be clearly a country-specific issue. These tests can therefore be used to gain a sense as to whether PC global inflation might help beyond the simple AR specification. In Online Supplement Section 6 we repeat the exercise without factors but using a grand average of country level inflations (which excludes the country of reference each time) as global inflation. What comes out is an overly suspicious abundant significance across most countries and all tests. Outliers and multicollinearity are likely to affect these results, as evident from the boxplots and pairwise correlation heatmap in Figure
14
and Figure
14
, respectively. It is therefore safe to say that the analysis including PC global inflation is much more trustworthy.

Figure 14:

Remark 4

We suggest the potential macroeconomics practitioners to interpret the outcomes of the 4 tests as some sort of “battery of checks”, to be used jointly in understanding whether the factor augmentation is worth to improve the quality of the forecast. Acknowledging that, as outlined above, each test has its own nuance,
g_{\widehat{f},1}
should be given more weight in the decision. This is because, as explained above, due the overlapping evaluation over the effective sample size for the case of
g_{\widehat{f},2}^{adj}
,
g_{\widehat{f},3}^{adj}
,
g_{\widehat{f},4}^{adj}
, some data is lost in the MSEs comparison, which does not occur for
g_{\widehat{f},1}
. As such, a joint significance of
g_{\widehat{f},1}
and at least one of
g_{\widehat{f},2}^{adj}
,
g_{\widehat{f},3}^{adj}
,
g_{\widehat{f},4}^{adj}
should possibly be taken as a solid indication that considering a factor-augmented specification can increase the forecast accuracy.

4.2
FRED-MD Forecasting

We continue the empirical exercise by considering more macroeconomic series, this time from an even larger pool of series. Table
4.2
collects the p-values for the tests of encompassing and forecast accuracy described in Section
2
, using the same tunings as in the previous application in Section
4.1
, for 14 important macroeconomic series taken from the FRED-MD dataset
McCracken & Ng, (2016)
(July 2024 vintage, starting January 1960). The FRED-MD dataset includes a wide range of U.S. macroeconomic series such as output (e.g., industrial production), income, labor market indicators (e.g., unemployment rate, payrolls), consumer and producer prices, money supply, interest rates, exchange rates, and financial variables like stock indices and spreads. These series capture economic activity, inflation dynamics, monetary policy, and financial market conditions. All series are cleaned and stationary transformed as prescribed by the Matlab routines provided with FRED-MD. After the necessary cleaning of NAs and outliers, the final dataset contains
117
series for
772
data points. Again we choose the tuning parameters in line with the simulations to balance the power and size (see our extensive simulations in the Online Supplement).

We forecast one-month-ahead these
14
macroeconomic series using the PC factors in the alternative forecasting specification (i.e.,
AR(1)
vs factor augmented
AR(1)
, where factors are estimated via
Bai & Ng, (2002)

IC_{p1}
with max number
=15
).
15
15

15

We choose to follow a common convention in the factor modeling literature by setting a relatively high maximum number of factors, from which the optimal number is selected using IC. The goal of this is to ensure that the true number of factors is not underestimated due to an arbitrarily restrictive upper bound.
16
16

16

We collect the full names of the
14
FRED-MD macroeconomic time series in the Online Supplement (Section 6, Table 6.2)

With an average of
7
estimated common factors, the results are overwhelmingly significant across the four tests.
Apart from “Real personal consumption expenditures” (DPCERA3M086SBEA), all the other series have at least one significant (at nominal level 5%) test statistic. This shows how even in a high(er) dimensional dataset such as the FRED-MD, these test statistics are very useful in assessing whether PC factors could be employed to improve the forecast of macroeconomic series.

5
Conclusion

We develop the theoretical framework that allows the forecast accuracy and encompassing tests proposed by
Pitarakis, (2023)
;
Pitarakis, (2025)
to be applied when the alternative forecasting model incorporates estimated PC factors. These factors can have loadings that are either strong or weak, whether homogeneously or heterogeneously distributed. Our theoretical findings are supported by both a Monte Carlo simulation and two empirical applications in macroeconomic forecasting.

Mathematical Supplement

Contents

Appendix 1
Notation and Assumptions

Throughout this supplement, we will use the following notation. Firstly,
a
is a scalar,
\mathbf{a}
is a vector and
\mathbf{A}_{t}
is a matrix with
t
rows. For any generic matrix
\mathbf{A}
, spectral norm is
\left\|\mathbf{A}\right\|_{sp}=\sqrt{\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\mathbf{A})}
, while
\|\mathbf{A}\|=\sqrt{\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\mathbf{A})}
is the Frobenius (Euclidean) norm with
\mathrm{tr}(.)
being the trace operator. Vectorization of a matrix
\mathbf{A}
is denoted by
\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{A})
, and
\otimes
represents Kronecker product. By
\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{A})
and
\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{A})
we denote the smallest and largest eigenvalues, respectively. Next,
\lfloor x\rfloor
represents the integer part of
x
, and
M
is a positive constant, which does not necessarily have the same value for various statements, while
\sup_{a\leq t\leq b}
(
\inf_{a\leq t\leq b}
) is supremum (infimum) over a discrete set
\{a,\ldots,b\}
. Moreover,
k_{0}
represents the in-sample observations, while
T-k_{0}=n
are the out-of-sample observations. Weak convergence and equality in distribution are denoted by
\Rightarrow
and
\overset{D}{=}
, respectively, while convergence in distribution and probability are given by
\to_{d}
and
\to_{p}
, respectively. Ultimately,
\widetilde{a}
and
\widehat{a}
are quantities estimated under the observed and estimated
\mathbf{f}_{t}
.

A.1 (Forecast Error)

i)

\{u_{t}\}
is a martingale difference sequence with respect to the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}=\sigma(\mathbf{z}_{t-1},\ldots,\allowbreak\mathbf{X}_{t-1},\ldots,y_{t-1},\ldots)
.

ii)

\mathbb{E}(u_{t}^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{t-1})=\sigma_{t}^{2}
,
\sup_{k_{0}\leq t\leq T-1}\sigma^{2}_{t}=O_{p}(1)
,
\sigma^{2}=\mathbb{E}(\sigma_{t}^{2})
.

iii)

\sup_{k_{0}\leq t\leq T-1}\mathbb{E}(u_{t}^{4})<M
.

iv)

u_{t}
is independent from all other model primitives for all
i,t,s
.

A.2 (Idiosyncratics)

i)

\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{E}_{t}|\mathbf{F}_{t},\boldsymbol{\Lambda})=\mathbf{0}_{t\times N}

ii)

\sup_{k_{0}\leq t\leq T-1}\mathbb{E}(|e_{i,t}|^{4})<M
for all
i,t
.

iii)

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(e_{i,t}e_{i,s}-\mathbb{E}(e_{i,t}e_{i,s})\right)^{4}\right]<M
.

iv)

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{s=1}^{T}[\mathbb{E}(e_{i,t}e_{i,s})]^{2}<M
(square summability).

v)

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{s_{1}=1}^{T}\sum_{s_{2}=1}^{T}\left|\mathrm{C}ov\left(e_{i,s_{1}}e_{j,s_{1}},e_{i,s_{2}}e_{j,s_{2}}\right)\right|=O_{p}(1)

vi)

Let
\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{e}_{t}\mathbf{e}_{t}^{\prime}|\mathbf{F}_{t},\boldsymbol{\Lambda})=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
, which is deterministic. There exists
m>0
and some positive
M
, such that
\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{e})>m
and
\left\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{e}\right\|_{sp}<M

vii)

\sup_{k_{0}\leq t\leq T-1}\left\|\mathbf{E}_{t}\right\|_{sp}=\max\{\sqrt{N},\sqrt{T}\}
.

A.3 (Factors, Loadings and Predictors)

i)

\sup_{k_{0}\leq t\leq T-1}\mathbb{E}(\left\|\mathbf{z}_{t}\right\|^{8})<M
, where
\mathbf{z}_{t}=(\mathbf{w}_{t}^{\prime},\mathbf{f}_{t}^{\prime})^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k+r}
.

ii)

\sup_{k_{0}\leq t\leq T-1}\left\|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{s=1}^{t}(\mathbf{z}_{s}\mathbf{z}_{s}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{z}})\right\|=O_{p}(T^{-1/2})
and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{z}}=\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{z}_{t}\mathbf{z}_{t}^{\prime})=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}}&\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}\\
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}^{\prime}&\mathbf{I}_{r}\end{bmatrix}
.

iii)

\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{s=1}^{t}\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{z}_{s}\mathbf{z}_{s}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{z}})
converges weakly.

iv)

(
Homogeneous
)
\left\|N^{-\alpha}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}\right\|=O_{p}(N^{-\alpha/2})
for a positive definite
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
as
N\to\infty
. (
Heterogeneous
)
\left\|\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}\right\|=O_{p}(N^{-\alpha_{r}/2})
for a positive definite
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
as
N\to\infty
. Both
N^{-\alpha}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
and
\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}
are diagonal and positive definite with eigenvalues ordered in a decreasing fashion. In both cases, the loadings are independent from
\mathbf{F}_{t}
for all
t
.

v)

(
Homogeneous
)
\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{N^{\alpha/2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{f}_{s}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}e_{i,t}\right)^{2}\right]<M
and
\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{N^{\alpha}T}}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}\mathbf{E}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\right\|=O_{p}(1)
for all
t,s
. (
Heterogeneous
)
\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}(N^{-1/2}\mathbf{B}_{N}\mathbf{f}_{s})^{\prime}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}e_{i,t}\right)^{2}\right]<M
and
\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{N^{\alpha_{1}}T}}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}\mathbf{E}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\right\|=O_{p}(1)
for all
t,s
.

vi)

(
Homogeneous
)
\mathbb{E}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{N^{\alpha/2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}e_{i,t}\right\|^{8}\right)<M
for all
t
. (
Heterogeneous
)
\mathbb{E}\left(\left\|\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}e_{i,t}\right\|^{8}\right)<M
for all
t
.

vii)

(
Homogeneous
)
\left\|\frac{1}{N^{\alpha}T}\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\prime}\mathbf{E}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\right\|=O_{p}(N^{-\alpha})+O_{p}(N^{-\alpha/2}T^{-1/2})
for all
i=1,\ldots,N
. (
Heterogeneous
)
\left\|\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\prime}\mathbf{E}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}\right\|=O_{p}(N^{-\alpha_{r}})+O_{p}\left(\sqrt{\frac{N^{\alpha_{1}}}{N^{\alpha_{r}}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\right)
for all
i=1,\ldots,N
.

viii)

\sup_{k_{0}\leq t\leq T-1}\|T^{-1/2}\sum_{s=1}^{t}\mathbf{f}_{s}e_{i,s}\|^{2}\leq M
for all
i=1,\ldots,N
and
\left\|\frac{1}{NT}\mathbf{e}_{s}^{\prime}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\prime}\mathbf{F}_{t}\right\|=O_{p}(N^{-1})+O_{p}(T^{-1})
for all
t,s
.

A.4 (
N,T
Expansion Rates)

i)

(Homogeneous)

\frac{N}{N^{\alpha}}\frac{1}{T^{1/4}}\to c>0
as
(N,T)\to\infty
.
(Heterogeneous)

\frac{N}{N^{\alpha_{r}}}\frac{1}{T^{1/4}}\to c>0
as
(N,T)\to\infty
for
\alpha
(
\alpha_{r}
)
\in(0.5,1)
.

ii)

(Homogeneous)

\sqrt{T}N^{-\alpha}\to 0
for
\alpha>0.5
.
(Heterogeneous)

\sqrt{T}N^{-\alpha_{r}}\to 0
for
\alpha_{r}>0.5
.

The assumptions are similar to the ones in
Gonçalves et al., (2017)
and
Bai & Ng, (2023)
as they are needed to accommodate recursive factor estimation and weaker loadings, respectively. Discussion of differences is in order. For instance,
A.1
ii) and iv) are absent in
Gonçalves et al., (2017)
, but they are fairly standard and allow to bring down moment requirements on factors and idiosynratics. Indeed, in the equivalent of
A.3
in
Gonçalves et al., (2017)
, 16th moments are required in parts i) and v). Note that we do not assume independence between the idiosyncratic components and the rest of the primitives. Otherwise, we would need only 4th moments.
A.2
is virtually the same as in the latter study, except for the lower moment requirement.
A.2
vii) is the same as in
Bai & Ng, (2023)
, but it is required to hold uniformly in
t
. We formulate it in terms of
T
, because
t=\lfloor rT\rfloor
for some
r\in(0,1)
due to the recursive setup. It is primarily used to simplify proofs when the loading weakness is heterogeneous, but it also helps to improve convergence rates.
A.3
i) - iii) ensure that the tests in
Pitarakis, (2023)
and
Pitarakis, (2025)
are asymptotically normal as required, but under lower-level conditions. For example, part iii) is similar to the one in
Clark & McCracken, (2001)
and
Stauskas & Westerlund, (2022)
, where
\{\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{z}_{t}\mathbf{z}_{t}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{z}})\}_{t=1}^{T}
follows a mixing sequence of a specific size (see
Hansen,, 1992
). In part iv), we treat the loadings as random and make them independent from
\mathbf{F}_{t}
in order to simplify some arguments. Alternatively, we can impose
\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{f}_{t}\mathbf{f}_{t}^{\prime}|\boldsymbol{\Lambda})=\mathbf{I}_{r}
. They can also be treated as fixed, similarly to
Gonçalves et al., (2017)
. Parts v) - vii) ensure that the feasible variance estimator of the tests approximate the infeasible counterparts (observed factors) well. Furthermore,
A.4
i) is needed to make convergence rates more transparent. Particularly, in the proofs we will need
\frac{N}{N^{\alpha}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}=o(1)
, but at the same time
T^{1/4}\frac{N}{N^{\alpha}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}=O(1)
. Thus, under
A.4
i),
\frac{N}{N^{\alpha}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}=O_{p}(T^{-1/4})
. Part ii) of the same assumption is identical to the one needed for inference in Lemma 4 of
Bai & Ng, (2023)
. Under
\alpha=1
(strong loadings), it naturally coincides with the requirement of
\sqrt{T}N^{-1}=o(1)
in
Gonçalves et al., (2017)
.

Appendix 2
Overview of the Tests

Remark 5

In this supplementary material, we use the notation
g_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,4
to denote our statistics for conciseness purposes only. The original notation in
Pitarakis, (2023)
and
Pitarakis, (2025)
uses
\varepsilon_{f}
,
z_{f}
and
\overline{z}_{f}
, respectively (and the respective subscripts for the variance estimators).

2.1
Tests for Forecast Encompassing and Accuracy

We consider in total 4 tests in total. One is for the forecast encompassing and two are for equal forecast accuracy. The test for forecast encompassing is given by
Pitarakis, (2023)
and it has the following form:

\displaystyle\mathrm{(i.)}\quad g_{f,1}=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\omega}_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}\widetilde{u}^{2}_{1,t+1}-\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{n}{m_{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}\widetilde{u}_{1,t+1}\widetilde{u}_{2,t+1}+\frac{n}{n-m_{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}+m_{0}}^{T-1}\widetilde{u}_{1,t+1}\widetilde{u}_{2,t+1}\right]\right),

(2.1)

where
k_{0}=\lfloor T\pi_{0}\rfloor
for
\pi_{0}\in(0,1
) and
m_{0}=\lfloor n\mu_{0}\rfloor=\lfloor(T-k_{0})\mu_{0}\rfloor
is a cut-off point to split the average for
\mu_{0}\in(0,1)
. We must require
\mu_{0}\neq 1/2
, as otherwise the asymptotic variance of the statistic would degenerate. Also,
\widetilde{\omega}_{1}
is the estimated standard deviation of the limiting distribution of the test statistic.
Two tests of forecasting accuracy come from
Pitarakis, (2025)
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{(ii.)}\quad g_{f,2}=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\omega}}_{2}\frac{n}{l^{0}_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{1}-1}\widetilde{u}^{2}_{1,t+1}-\frac{l^{0}_{1}}{l^{0}_{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{2}-1}\widetilde{u}^{2}_{2,t+1}\right)

(2.2)

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{(iii.)}\quad g_{f,3}=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\omega}}_{3}\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{1}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}g_{f,2}(l_{1},\lfloor n\lambda^{0}_{2}\rfloor).

(2.3)

Here,
l^{0}_{j}=\lfloor n\lambda_{j}^{0}\rfloor
for
j=1,2
and
\lambda_{j}^{0}\in(0,1)
, which represent two portions of the out-of-sample period of which the forecast errors of both models are compared. Note that we can have
l^{0}_{1}>l_{2}^{0}
or vice-versa, which means that both portions are overlapping. However, we cannot have equality in order to avoid the asymptotic degeneracy of the variance. Note that (
2.3
) is simply an average of (
2.2
) over some chosen feasible set of
l_{1}
for the fixed
l_{2}^{0}
(or, fixed
\lambda_{2}^{0}
). The tuning parameter
\tau_{0}\in(0,1)
helps to pick that set. Intuition behind the averaging is the following: if
l_{2}^{0}
is fixed and
l_{1}
changes, then the MSE of the restricted model accumulates. In effect, possible choices of
l_{1}
are aggregated. Lastly,
\widetilde{\omega}_{2}
and
\widetilde{\omega}_{3}
represent estimators of the standard deviations of both test statistics.

The next test is an alternative to (iii.). It is based on averaging over
l_{2}
while the
l_{1}^{0}
is fixed, such that the MSE of the unrestricted model accumulates. That is

\displaystyle\mathrm{(iv.)}\quad g_{f,4}(\tau_{0},\lambda^{0}_{1})=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\omega}}_{4}\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{2}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}g_{f,2}(\lfloor n\lambda^{0}_{1}\rfloor,l_{2}).

(2.4)

Note that this statistic is not present in
Pitarakis, (2025)
, although it is suggested that alternative averaging choices can be made. In total, we can summarize both (
2.3
) and (
2.4
) as

\displaystyle g_{p,f}(\tau_{0},\lambda^{0}_{j})=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\omega}}_{p}\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{j}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}g_{f,2}(\lfloor n\lambda^{0}_{j}\rfloor,l_{k}),

(2.5)

where
p=3,4
,
j,k=1,2
with
j\neq k
.

2.2
On the Infeasible Statistics

The infeasible statistics (i.e., observed
\mathbf{f}_{t}
) can be decomposed into two components, such that for
j=1,\ldots,4
we have

\displaystyle g_{f,j}=\widetilde{\omega}_{j}^{-1}(g_{f,j,1}+g_{f,j,2}),

(2.6)

where
g_{f,j,1}
generates the distribution under the null. The infeasible variance estimator is
\widetilde{\omega}_{j}^{2}=\widetilde{\mathbb{V}ar}(g_{j})
. In particular, as
T\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\omega}_{j}^{-1}g_{f,j,1}\to_{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1).

(2.7)

At the same time,
g_{f,j,2}
is negligible or generates statistical power under the null and the alternative, respectively. Firstly, we will explore
g_{f,j,1}
and then show that
g_{f,j,2}\to_{p}f_{j}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0})=0
if
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}=\mathbf{0}_{r}
.

2.2.1
The Null Distribution

In this section, we will analyze the asymptotic null distributions of the infeasible statistics under our assumptions, whereas in
Pitarakis, (2023)
and
Pitarakis, (2025)
they are obtained under high-level conditions. The power terms will be explored in the upcoming section. In the proof, we will for simplicity let
\mathbb{E}(u_{t}^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{t-1})=\sigma^{2}
. We will comment on what changes under conditional heteroskedasticity. The key is that
u^{2}_{t+1}-\sigma^{2}
is no longer a martingale difference under conditional heteroskedasticity. Note how
\mathbb{E}[(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})(u_{t}^{2}-\sigma^{2})]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{t})(u_{t}^{2}-\sigma^{2})]\neq 0
unless
\mathbb{E}(u_{t+1}^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{t})=\sigma^{2}
.

Proposition 3

Under Assumptions A.1 and A.3, as
T\to\infty
one has

\displaystyle\widetilde{\omega}_{j}^{-1}g_{f,j,1}\to_{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1)

for
j=1,\ldots,4
.

Proof
. Let
j=1
. In particular,

\displaystyle g_{f,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\omega}_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}\widetilde{u}^{2}_{1,t+1}-\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{n}{m_{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}\widetilde{u}_{1,t+1}\widetilde{u}_{2,t+1}+\frac{n}{n-m_{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}+m_{0}}^{T-1}\widetilde{u}_{1,t+1}\widetilde{u}_{2,t+1}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\omega}_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}u_{t+1}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{n}{m_{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}u_{t+1}^{2}+\frac{n}{n-m_{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}+m_{0}}^{T-1}u_{t+1}^{2}\right]\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\widetilde{\omega}_{1}}\Bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{1,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})-\frac{1}{2}\Bigg[\frac{n}{m_{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}(\widetilde{u}_{1,t+1}\widetilde{u}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})

\displaystyle+\frac{n}{n-m_{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}+m_{0}}^{T-1}(\widetilde{u}_{1,t+1}\widetilde{u}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})\Bigg]\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\omega}_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})-\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{n}{m_{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})+\frac{n}{n-m_{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}+m_{0}}^{T-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})\right]\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\widetilde{\omega}_{1}}\Bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{1,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})-\frac{1}{2}\Bigg[\frac{n}{m_{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}(\widetilde{u}_{1,t+1}\widetilde{u}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})

\displaystyle+\frac{n}{n-m_{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}+m_{0}}^{T-1}(\widetilde{u}_{1,t+1}\widetilde{u}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})\Bigg]\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\omega}_{1}^{-1}(g_{f,1,1}+g_{f,1,2}),

(2.8)

where
g_{f,1,1}
generates the asymptotic distribution and
g_{f,1,2}
gives the statistical power under the alternative, but it is negligible under the null. We start with
g_{f,1,1}
. In particular, notice how by splitting the first term appropriately, we obtain

\displaystyle g_{f,1,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})-\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{n}{m_{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})+\frac{n}{n-m_{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}+m_{0}}^{T-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}+m_{0}}^{T-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{n}{m_{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})+\frac{n}{n-m_{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}+m_{0}}^{T-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})\right]

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\frac{n}{m_{0}}\right)\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})+\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\frac{n}{n-m_{0}}\right)\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}+m_{0}}^{T-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\frac{n}{m_{0}}\right)\sqrt{\frac{T}{n}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})+\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\frac{n}{n-m_{0}}\right)\sqrt{\frac{T}{n}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=k_{0}+m_{0}}^{T-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2}),

where under our martingale difference assumption and existence of the fourth moment, we have that both
\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})
and
\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=k_{0}+m_{0}}^{T-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})
are
O_{p}(1)
, plus they are uncorrelated. Therefore, by using the definitions of the tuning parameters and denoting
\phi^{2}=\mathbb{V}ar(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})
, we obtain as
T\to\infty

\displaystyle g_{f,1,1}

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{1}{2\mu_{0}}\right)\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\pi_{0}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=\lfloor T\pi_{o}\rfloor}^{\lfloor T\pi_{o}\rfloor+\lfloor(T-k_{0})\mu_{0}\rfloor-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle+\left(1-\frac{1}{2(1-\mu_{0})}\right)\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\pi_{0}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=\lfloor T\pi_{o}\rfloor+\lfloor(T-k_{0})\mu_{0}\rfloor}^{T-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left(1-\frac{1}{2\mu_{0}}\right)\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\pi_{0}}}\phi\int_{s=\pi_{0}}^{\pi_{0}+\mu_{0}-\pi_{0}\mu_{0}}dW_{u}(s)

\displaystyle+\left(1-\frac{1}{2(1-\mu_{0})}\right)\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\pi_{0}}}\phi\int_{s=\pi_{0}+\mu_{0}-\pi_{0}\mu_{0}}^{1}dW_{u}(s)

\displaystyle\overset{D}{=}\frac{2\mu_{0}-1}{2\mu_{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\pi_{0}}}\phi\mathcal{N}\left(0,\mu_{0}(1-\pi_{0})\right)+\frac{1-2\mu_{0}}{2(1-\mu_{0})}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\pi_{0}}}\phi\mathcal{N}\left(0,(1-\pi_{0})(1-\mu_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\overset{D}{=}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\phi^{2}\frac{(1-2\mu_{0})^{2}}{4\mu_{0}}\right)+\mathcal{N}\left(0,\phi^{2}\frac{(1-2\mu_{0})^{2}}{4(1-\mu_{0})}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{D}{=}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\phi^{2}\frac{(1-2\mu_{0})^{2}(1-\mu_{0})+(1-2\mu_{0})^{2}\mu_{0}}{4\mu_{0}(1-\mu_{0})}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{D}{=}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\phi^{2}\frac{(1-2\mu_{0})^{2}}{4\mu_{0}(1-\mu_{0})}\right),

(2.10)

where we used the fact that Brownian increments are normally distributed for the fixed tuning parameters. Also, we merged the normal variates because they are uncorrelated, and hence independent. This implies that as
T\to\infty

\displaystyle\widetilde{\omega}_{1}^{-1}g_{f,1,1}\to_{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1).

(2.11)

Note that if
\mathbb{E}(u_{t}^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{t-1})=\sigma_{t}^{2}
, then
\phi^{2}
should be interpreted as a long-run variance, because
\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{s=1}^{t}(u_{s+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})
still generates Brownian motion under ARCH/GARCH errors (see
Pitarakis,, 2025
). Also,
\widetilde{\omega}^{2}_{1}
should have a HAC correction. The same applies for the remaining statistics. Further we go the the distribution part under the null of
g_{f,2}
, that is
j=2
. Note how

\displaystyle g_{f,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\omega}}_{2}\frac{n}{l^{0}_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{1}-1}\widetilde{u}^{2}_{1,t+1}-\frac{l^{0}_{1}}{l^{0}_{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{2}-1}\widetilde{u}^{2}_{2,t+1}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\omega}}_{2}\frac{n}{l^{0}_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{1}-1}u_{t+1}^{2}-\frac{l^{0}_{1}}{l^{0}_{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{2}-1}u_{t+1}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\widetilde{\omega}}_{2}\frac{n}{l^{0}_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{1}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{1,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})-\frac{l^{0}_{1}}{l^{0}_{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{2}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})\right)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\omega}_{2}^{-1}(g_{f,2,1}+g_{f,2,2}),

(2.12)

where
g_{f,2,1}
is the component that is responsible for the distribution under the null. In particular,

\displaystyle g_{f,2,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{l^{0}_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{1}-1}u_{t+1}^{2}-\frac{l^{0}_{1}}{l^{0}_{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{2}-1}u_{t+1}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{l^{0}_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{1}-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})-\frac{l^{0}_{1}}{l^{0}_{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{2}-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{n}{l^{0}_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{1}-1}\sigma^{2}-\frac{l^{0}_{1}}{l^{0}_{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{2}-1}\sigma^{2}\right)=g_{f,2,1,1}+g_{f,2,1,2},

(2.13)

where
g_{z,11}
is precisely zero under homoskedasticity:

\displaystyle g_{f,2,1,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{\lfloor n\lambda^{0}_{1}\rfloor}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\lfloor n\lambda_{1}^{0}\rfloor-\frac{\lfloor n\lambda_{1}^{0}\rfloor}{\lfloor n\lambda_{2}^{0}\rfloor}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\lfloor n\lambda_{2}^{0}\rfloor\right)\sigma^{2}=\frac{n}{\lfloor n\lambda^{0}_{1}\rfloor}\sqrt{n}\left(\frac{\lfloor n\lambda_{1}^{0}\rfloor}{n}-\frac{\lfloor n\lambda_{1}^{0}\rfloor}{n}\right)\sigma^{2}=0.

(2.14)

Next, by using
\frac{n}{\lfloor n\lambda^{0}_{1}\rfloor}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}^{0}}+O(n^{-1})
,
\frac{\lfloor n\lambda^{0}_{1}\rfloor}{\lfloor n\lambda^{0}_{2}\rfloor}=\frac{\lambda_{1}^{0}}{\lambda_{2}^{0}}+O(n^{-1})
and
\sqrt{\frac{T}{n}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\pi_{0}}}+O(n^{-1})

17
17

17

For this and similar terms we use the fundamental relationship
\sup_{1\leq t\leq T}\sup_{c\in(0,1)}\left|\left(\frac{t}{T}\right)^{k}-c^{k}\right|=O(T^{-1})
for all finite
k
(see p. 512 in
Moon & Phillips,, 2004
).
and boundedness of the sums due to the martingale difference assumption, we get as
T\to\infty

\displaystyle g_{f,2,1,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{l^{0}_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{1}-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})-\frac{l^{0}_{1}}{l^{0}_{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{2}-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}^{0}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\pi_{0}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=\lfloor T\pi_{0}\rfloor}^{\lfloor T\pi_{0}\rfloor+\lfloor(T-k_{0})\lambda_{1}^{0}\rfloor-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})-\frac{\lambda_{1}^{0}}{\lambda_{2}^{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\pi_{0}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=\lfloor T\pi_{0}\rfloor}^{\lfloor T\pi_{0}\rfloor+\lfloor(T-k_{0})\lambda_{2}^{0}\rfloor-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})\right)

\displaystyle+O(n^{-1})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}^{0}}\left(\frac{\phi}{\sqrt{1-\pi_{0}}}\int_{s=\pi_{0}}^{\pi_{0}+\lambda_{1}^{0}(1-\pi_{0})}dW_{u}(s)-\frac{\lambda_{1}^{0}}{\lambda_{2}^{0}}\frac{\phi}{\sqrt{1-\pi_{0}}}\int_{s=\pi_{0}}^{\pi_{0}+\lambda_{2}^{0}(1-\pi_{0})}dW_{u}(s)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\phi}{\lambda_{1}^{0}}\left(W_{u}(\lambda_{1}^{0})-\frac{\lambda_{1}^{0}}{\lambda_{2}^{0}}W_{u}(\lambda_{2}^{0})\right)=\phi\left(W_{u}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}^{0}}\right)-W_{u}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{2}^{0}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\overset{D}{=}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\phi^{2}\left|\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}^{0}}-\frac{1}{\lambda_{2}^{0}}\right|\right)

\displaystyle\overset{D}{=}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\phi^{2}\frac{|\lambda_{1}^{0}-\lambda_{2}^{0}|}{\lambda_{1}^{0}\lambda_{2}^{0}}\right)

(2.15)

by the properties of the distribution of Brownian increments and the fact that
\lambda_{1}^{0}
and
\lambda_{2}^{0}
are positive. Again, this implies that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\omega}_{2}^{-1}g_{f,2,1}\to_{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1).

(2.16)

Lastly, we work with
j=2,3
. Respectively, by using the steps employed in (
2.13
) and (
2.14
), we obtain

\displaystyle g_{f,3}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\omega}}_{3}\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{1}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}g_{f,2}(l_{1},\lfloor n\lambda^{0}_{2}\rfloor)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\omega}}_{3}\Bigg[\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{1}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}\frac{n}{l_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l_{1}-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})-\frac{l_{1}}{l^{0}_{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{2}-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{1}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}\frac{n}{l_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l_{1}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{1,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})-\frac{l_{1}}{l^{0}_{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{2}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})\right)\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\omega}_{3}^{-1}(g_{f,3,1}+g_{f,3,2}),

(2.17)

and

\displaystyle g_{f,4}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\omega}}_{4}\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{2}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}g_{f,2}(\lfloor n\lambda^{0}_{1}\rfloor,l_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\omega}}_{4}\Bigg[\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{2}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}\frac{n}{l_{1}^{0}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{1}-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})-\frac{l^{0}_{1}}{l_{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l_{2}-1}(u_{t+1}^{2}-\sigma^{2})\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{2}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}\frac{n}{l_{1}^{0}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{1}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{1,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})-\frac{l^{0}_{1}}{l_{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l_{2}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})\right)\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\omega}_{4}^{-1}(g_{f,4,1}+g_{f,4,2})

(2.18)

where
l_{1}=\lfloor n\lambda_{1}\rfloor
and
l_{2}=\lfloor n\lambda_{2}\rfloor
with either
\lambda_{1}
or
\lambda_{2}
varying now depending on the averaging choice. Clearly,
g_{f,3,1}
(
g_{f,4,1}
) is responsible for the distribution under the null, whereas
g_{f,3,2}
(
g_{f,4,2}
) will stay negligible under the null as averaging over the out-of-sample (sub)set will not change the order of the remainder. Clearly, because
\lambda_{2}^{0}
is fixed in (
2.17
), by using the results in (
2.15
), we have that as
T\to\infty

\displaystyle g_{f,3,1}\Rightarrow\frac{\phi}{1-\tau_{0}}\int_{\lambda_{1}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\left(\frac{W_{u}(\lambda_{1})}{\lambda_{1}}-\frac{W_{u}(\lambda_{2}^{0})}{\lambda_{2}^{0}}\right)d\lambda_{1},

(2.19)

whereas, because
\lambda_{1}^{0}
is fixed in (
2.18
), we have

\displaystyle g_{f,4,1}\Rightarrow\frac{\phi}{1-\tau_{0}}\int_{\lambda_{2}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\left(\frac{W_{u}(\lambda^{0}_{1})}{\lambda^{0}_{1}}-\frac{W_{u}(\lambda_{2})}{\lambda_{2}}\right)d\lambda_{2},

(2.20)

which both are normal variables. By using
\mathrm{C}ov(W_{u}(s_{1}),W_{u}(s_{2}))=\mathrm{min}(s_{1},s_{2})
, we can obtain the variance of
g_{f,4,1}
by the Fubini’s Theorem:

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}ar(g_{f,4,1})

\displaystyle=\omega_{4}^{2}=\frac{\phi^{2}}{(1-\tau_{0})^{2}}

\displaystyle\times\int_{s_{1}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\int_{s_{2}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\mathbb{E}\left(\left[\frac{W_{u}(\lambda_{1}^{0})}{\lambda_{1}^{0}}\right]^{2}-\frac{W_{u}(\lambda_{1}^{0})}{\lambda_{1}^{0}}\frac{W_{u}(s_{2})}{s_{2}}-\frac{W_{u}(s_{1})}{s_{1}}\frac{W_{u}(\lambda_{1}^{0})}{\lambda_{1}^{0}}+\frac{W_{u}(s_{1})}{s_{1}}\frac{W_{u}(s_{2})}{s_{2}}\right)ds_{2}ds_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\phi^{2}}{(1-\tau_{0})^{2}}\times\int_{s_{1}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\int_{s_{2}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}^{0}}-\frac{\mathrm{min}(\lambda_{1}^{0},s_{2})}{\lambda_{1}^{0}s_{2}}-\frac{\mathrm{min}(\lambda_{1}^{0},s_{1})}{\lambda_{1}^{0}s_{1}}+\frac{\mathrm{min}(s_{1},s_{2})}{s_{1}s_{2}}\right)ds_{2}ds_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\phi^{2}}{(1-\tau_{0})^{2}}\times\int_{s_{1}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\int_{s_{2}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}^{0}}ds_{2}ds_{1}

\displaystyle+\frac{\phi^{2}}{(1-\tau_{0})^{2}}\times\int_{s_{1}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\int_{s_{2}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\left(-\frac{\mathrm{min}(\lambda_{1}^{0},s_{2})}{\lambda_{1}^{0}s_{2}}-\frac{\mathrm{min}(\lambda_{1}^{0},s_{1})}{\lambda_{1}^{0}s_{1}}+\frac{\mathrm{min}(s_{1},s_{2})}{s_{1}s_{2}}\right)ds_{2}ds_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\phi^{2}}{\lambda_{1}^{0}}+\frac{\phi^{2}}{(1-\tau_{0})^{2}}(-I-II+III),

(2.21)

where the solution of
I-III
will depend on whether
\lambda_{1}^{0}\leq\tau_{0}
or vice versa. Let
\lambda_{1}^{0}\leq\tau_{0}
, then
\mathrm{min}(\lambda_{1}^{0},s_{1})=\mathrm{min}(\lambda_{1}^{0},s_{2})=\lambda_{1}^{0}
, because both
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
belong to
[\tau_{0},1]
. Then

\displaystyle I=\int_{s_{1}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\int_{s_{2}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{min}(\lambda_{1}^{0},s_{2})}{\lambda_{1}^{0}s_{2}}ds_{2}ds_{1}=\int_{s_{1}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\int_{s_{2}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\frac{1}{s_{2}}ds_{2}ds_{1}=-(1-\tau_{0})\ln(\tau_{0}),

(2.22)

which is positive. Next,

\displaystyle II=\int_{s_{1}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\int_{s_{2}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{min}(\lambda_{1}^{0},s_{1})}{\lambda_{1}^{0}s_{1}}ds_{2}ds_{1}=\int_{s_{1}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\int_{s_{2}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\frac{1}{s_{1}}ds_{2}ds_{1}=-(1-\tau_{0})\ln(\tau_{0})

(2.23)

by symmetry. Lastly, due to independence of coordinates:

\displaystyle III=\int_{s_{1}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\int_{s_{2}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{min}(s_{1},s_{2})}{s_{1}s_{2}}ds_{2}ds_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{s_{2}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\int_{s_{1}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{min}(s_{1},s_{2})}{s_{1}s_{2}}ds_{1}ds_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{s_{2}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\left(\underbrace{\int_{s_{1}=\tau_{0}}^{s_{2}}\frac{s_{1}}{s_{1}s_{2}}ds_{1}}_{s_{1}\leq s_{2}}+\underbrace{\int_{s_{1}=s_{2}}^{1}\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}s_{2}}ds_{1}}_{s_{2}\leq s_{1}}\right)ds_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{s_{2}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\left(\frac{s_{2}-\tau_{0}}{s_{2}}-\ln(s_{2})\right)ds_{2}

\displaystyle=(1-\tau_{0})-\tau_{0}(\ln(1)-\ln(\tau_{0}))-\int_{s_{2}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\ln(s_{2})ds_{2}

\displaystyle=(1-\tau_{0})-\tau_{0}(\ln(1)-\ln(\tau_{0}))-(-1-\tau_{0}\ln(\tau_{0})+\tau_{0})

\displaystyle=2(1-\tau_{0})+2\tau_{0}\ln(\tau_{0}).

(2.24)

Hence, if
\lambda_{1}^{0}\leq\tau_{0}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}ar(g_{f,4,1})=\phi^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}^{0}}+\frac{2(1-\tau_{0})+2\tau_{0}\ln(\tau_{0})+2(1-\tau_{0})\ln(\tau_{0})}{(1-\tau_{0})^{2}}\right)=\phi^{2}\frac{(1-\tau_{0})^{2}+2\lambda_{1}^{0}(1-\tau_{0}+\ln(\tau_{0}))}{\lambda_{1}^{0}(1-\tau_{0})^{2}}.

(2.25)

Now, we let
\lambda_{1}^{0}>\tau_{0}
. Clearly, we still have
III=2(1-\tau_{0})+2\tau_{0}\ln(\tau_{0})
since it is not a function of
\lambda_{1}^{0}
. However,

\displaystyle I=\int_{s_{1}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\int_{s_{2}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{min}(\lambda_{1}^{0},s_{2})}{\lambda_{1}^{0}s_{2}}ds_{2}ds_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{s_{1}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\left(\int_{s_{2}=\tau_{0}}^{\lambda_{1}^{0}}\frac{s_{2}}{s_{2}\lambda_{1}^{0}}ds_{2}+\int_{s_{2}=\lambda_{1}^{0}}^{1}\frac{\lambda_{1}^{0}}{s_{2}\lambda_{1}^{0}}ds_{2}\right)ds_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{s_{1}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}^{0}}(\lambda_{1}^{0}-\tau_{0})-\ln(\lambda_{1}^{0})\right)ds_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\lambda_{1}^{0}-\tau_{0})(1-\tau_{0})}{\lambda_{1}^{0}}-\ln(\lambda_{1}^{0})(1-\tau_{0}),

(2.26)

while by changing integration order

\displaystyle II=\int_{s_{1}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\int_{s_{2}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{min}(\lambda_{1}^{0},s_{1})}{\lambda_{1}^{0}s_{1}}ds_{2}ds_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{s_{2}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\int_{s_{1}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{min}(\lambda_{1}^{0},s_{1})}{\lambda_{1}^{0}s_{1}}ds_{1}ds_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{s_{2}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\left(\int_{s_{1}=\tau_{0}}^{\lambda_{1}^{0}}\frac{s_{1}}{s_{1}\lambda_{1}^{0}}ds_{1}+\int_{s_{1}=\lambda_{1}^{0}}^{1}\frac{\lambda_{1}^{0}}{s_{1}\lambda_{1}^{0}}ds_{1}\right)ds_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\lambda_{1}^{0}-\tau_{0})(1-\tau_{0})}{\lambda_{1}^{0}}-\ln(\lambda_{1}^{0})(1-\tau_{0})

(2.27)

by symmetry. Therefore, when
\lambda_{1}^{0}>\tau_{0}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}ar(g_{f,4,1})

\displaystyle=\phi^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}^{0}}+\frac{2(1-\tau_{0})+2\tau_{0}\ln(\tau_{0})}{(1-\tau_{0})^{2}}+\frac{2\ln(\lambda_{1}^{0})(1-\tau_{0})}{(1-\tau_{0})^{2}}-\frac{2(\lambda_{1}^{0}-\tau_{0})(1-\tau_{0})}{\lambda_{1}^{0}(1-\tau_{0})^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\phi^{2}\frac{1-\tau_{0}^{2}+2\lambda_{1}^{0}((1-\tau_{0})\ln(\lambda_{1}^{0})+\tau_{0}\ln(\tau_{0}))}{\lambda_{1}^{0}(1-\tau_{0})^{2}}.

(2.28)

Note that
\mathbb{V}ar(g_{f,4,1})
is symmetric to the variance of
g_{f,3,1}
which was demonstrated in Proposition 2 in
Pitarakis, (2025)
, because now it is the fixed
\lambda_{1}^{0}
that enters the formulas. In summary, for
j=3,4
and
p,q=1,2
, we have

\displaystyle\omega_{j,p}^{2}=\begin{cases}\phi^{2}\frac{(1-\tau_{0})^{2}+2\lambda_{q}^{0}(1-\tau_{0}+\ln(\tau_{0}))}{\lambda_{q}^{0}(1-\tau_{0})^{2}}\hskip 5.69054pt\text{if}\hskip 5.69054pt\lambda_{q}^{0}\leq\tau_{0},\\
\phi^{2}\frac{1-\tau_{0}^{2}+2\lambda_{q}^{0}((1-\tau_{0})\ln(\lambda_{q}^{0})+\tau_{0}\ln(\tau_{0}))}{\lambda_{q}^{0}(1-\tau_{0})^{2}}\hskip 5.69054pt\text{if}\hskip 5.69054pt\lambda_{q}^{0}>\tau_{0}\end{cases}

(2.29)

This implies that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\omega}_{3}^{-1}g_{f,3,1}\to_{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1),

(2.30)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\omega}_{4}^{-1}g_{f,4,1}\to_{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1)

(2.31)

as
T\to\infty
.

2.2.2
The Local Alternative

In this section we will briefly explore the local power generating terms
g_{f,j,2}
for
j=1,\ldots,4
.

Proposition 4

Under Assumptions A.1 and A.3, as
T\to\infty
one has

\displaystyle g_{f,j,2}\to_{p}\sqrt{1-\pi_{0}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}(\mathbf{I}_{r}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}_{\mathbf{w}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}})\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}

for
j=1,\ldots,4
.

Proof
. Let
j=1
. Note how

\displaystyle g_{f,1,2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{1,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})-

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Bigg[\frac{n}{m_{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}(\widetilde{u}_{1,t+1}\widetilde{u}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})

\displaystyle+\frac{n}{n-m_{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}+m_{0}}^{T-1}(\widetilde{u}_{1,t+1}\widetilde{u}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})\Bigg],

(2.32)

where

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{1,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}\mathbf{f}_{t}\mathbf{f}_{t}^{\prime}\right)\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}+\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}T^{1/4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\prime}\mathbf{w}_{t}\mathbf{w}_{t}^{\prime}T^{1/4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle-2\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}T^{1/4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\prime}\mathbf{w}_{t}\mathbf{f}_{t}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}-\frac{2}{T^{1/4}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}T^{1/4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\prime}\mathbf{w}_{t}u_{t+1}

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{T^{1/4}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}\mathbf{f}_{t}u_{t+1}.

(2.33)

Clearly,

\displaystyle T^{1/4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{t}\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}\mathbf{w}_{s}\mathbf{w}_{s}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{t}\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}\mathbf{w}_{s}\mathbf{f}_{s}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}+T^{-1/4}\frac{T}{t}\left(\frac{1}{t}\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}\mathbf{w}_{s}\mathbf{w}_{s}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}\mathbf{w}_{s}u_{s+1}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}+O_{p}(T^{-1/4})

(2.34)

uniformly in
t
since
\sup_{k_{0}\leq t\leq T-1}\frac{T}{t}=\sup_{s\in[\pi_{0},1]}s^{-1}+O(T^{-1})
. This and the fact that
\{\mathbf{z}_{s}u_{s+1}\}_{s=1}^{t-1}
is a heterogeneous MDS sequence immediately imply that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{2}{T^{1/4}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}T^{1/4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\prime}\mathbf{w}_{t}u_{t+1}\right|=O_{p}(T^{-1/4}),

(2.35)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{2}{T^{1/4}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}\mathbf{f}_{t}u_{t+1}\right|\leq\frac{2}{T^{1/4}}\left\|\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}\right\|\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}\mathbf{f}_{t}u_{t+1}\right\|=O_{p}(T^{-1/4}).

(2.36)

Note that (
2.35
) - (
2.36
) hold under ARCH/GARCH effects since

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}\mathbf{f}_{t}u_{t+1}\right\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{n}{T}\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}\sum_{s=k_{0}}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbf{f}_{t}^{\prime}\mathbf{f}_{s}u_{t+1}u_{s+1}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{n}{T}\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbf{f}_{t}^{\prime}\mathbf{f}_{t}\mathbb{E}(u_{t+1}^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{n}{T}\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left(\sigma_{t+1}^{2}\left\|\mathbf{f}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{n}{T}\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}(\sigma_{t+1}^{2})\mathbb{E}\left(\left\|\mathbf{f}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2}\left(\frac{n}{T}\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left(\left\|\mathbf{f}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right)=O(1),

(2.37)

where we were able to split the expectation, because
\sigma^{2}_{t}
is independent from other model primitives. Next,

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}T^{1/4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\prime}\mathbf{w}_{t}\mathbf{w}_{t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle T^{1/4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta})=\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}T^{1/4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}}T^{1/4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}\left[T^{1/4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta})\otimes T^{1/4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]^{\prime}n^{-1/2}\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{w}_{t}\mathbf{w}_{t}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}})}_{O_{p}(n^{-1/2})}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}_{\mathbf{w}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}_{\mathbf{w}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1-\pi_{0}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}_{\mathbf{w}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}+o_{p}(1)

(2.38)

by the same argument as in Lemma A.2 of
Stauskas & Westerlund, (2022)
. In particular,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}\left[T^{1/4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta})\otimes T^{1/4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]^{\prime}

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{w}_{t}\mathbf{w}_{t}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\int_{s=\pi_{0}}^{1}\left[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}\otimes\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}\right]^{\prime}d\mathbf{B}(s)+b,

(2.39)

where
\mathbf{B}(s)
is a Brownian motion and
b
represents a bias term induced by the time dependence of
\mathbf{z}_{t}
. Similarly,

\displaystyle 2\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}T^{1/4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\prime}\mathbf{w}_{t}\mathbf{f}_{t}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}T^{1/4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{2\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}\left[\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}\otimes T^{1/4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]^{\prime}n^{-1/2}\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{w}_{t}\mathbf{f}_{t}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}})}_{O_{p}(n^{-1/2})}

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{1-\pi_{0}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}_{\mathbf{w}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}+o_{p}(1).

(2.40)

Lastly,
\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{T-1}\mathbf{f}_{t}\mathbf{f}_{t}^{\prime}\right)\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}=\sqrt{1-\pi_{0}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}+O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
. The component that remains is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}(\widetilde{u}_{1,t+1}\widetilde{u}_{2,t+1}

\displaystyle-u_{t+1}^{2})=\frac{1}{T^{1/4}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}T^{1/4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\prime}\mathbf{w}_{t}u_{t+1}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{t}-\delta)^{\prime}\mathbf{z}_{t}u_{t+1}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{T^{1/4}}\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{t}-\delta)^{\prime}\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbf{z}_{t}\mathbf{f}_{t}^{\prime}\right)\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}-\frac{1}{T^{1/4}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}\mathbf{f}_{t}u_{t+1}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{T^{1/4}}\underbrace{\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}\left[\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}\otimes\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{t}-\delta)\right]^{\prime}n^{-1/2}\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{z}_{t}\mathbf{f}_{t}^{\prime}-\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbf{z}_{t}\mathbf{f}_{t}^{\prime}\right))}_{O_{p}(n^{-1/2})}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{T^{1/4}}\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{t}-\delta)^{\prime}\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{z}_{t}\mathbf{w}_{t}^{\prime})T^{1/4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{T^{1/4}}\underbrace{\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}\left[T^{1/4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta})\otimes\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{t}-\delta)\right]^{\prime}n^{-1/2}\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{z}_{t}\mathbf{w}_{t}^{\prime}-\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{z}_{t}\mathbf{w}_{t}^{\prime}))}_{O_{p}(n^{-1/2})}

\displaystyle=O_{p}(T^{-1/4})

(2.41)

by the same arguments as in (
2.35
) and (
2.36
) since
\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{t}-\delta)^{\prime}\mathbf{z}_{t}u_{t+1}\right\|=O_{p}(1)
by the MDS sequence assumption, and

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{T^{1/4}}\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{t}-\delta)^{\prime}\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbf{z}_{t}\mathbf{f}_{t}^{\prime}\right)\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{T^{1/4}}\sup_{k_{0}\leq t\leq T-1}\left\|\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{t}-\delta)\right\|\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}\left\|\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbf{z}_{t}\mathbf{f}_{t}^{\prime}\right)\right\|\left\|\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}\right\|

\displaystyle=O_{p}(T^{-1/4}).

(2.42)

Also,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}\left[T^{1/4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta})\otimes\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{t}-\delta)\right]^{\prime}

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{z}_{t}\mathbf{w}_{t}^{\prime}-\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{z}_{t}\mathbf{w}_{t}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\int_{s=\pi_{0}}^{\pi_{0}+\mu_{0}}\left[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}\otimes\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{W}(s))\right]^{\prime}d\mathbf{V}(s)+b

(2.43)

for two uncorrelated Brownian motions
\mathbf{W}(s)
and
\mathbf{V}(s)
, and so

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{T^{1/4}}\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+m_{0}-1}\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{t}-\delta)^{\prime}\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{z}_{t}\mathbf{w}_{t}^{\prime})T^{1/4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta})\right|=O_{p}(T^{-1/4}),

(2.44)

as well. By the same steps, by adjusting the summation limits, we can show that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}+m_{0}}^{T-1}(\widetilde{u}_{1,t+1}\widetilde{u}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})\right|=O_{p}(T^{-1/4})

(2.45)

as well. In summary, we have that as
T\to\infty
,

\displaystyle g_{f,1,2}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1-\pi_{0}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}+\sqrt{1-\pi_{0}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}_{\mathbf{w}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}

\displaystyle-2\sqrt{1-\pi_{0}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}_{\mathbf{w}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle\to_{p}\sqrt{1-\pi_{0}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}(\mathbf{I}_{r}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}_{\mathbf{w}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}})\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}.

(2.46)

Based on these results, we immediately obtain as
T\to\infty
for
j=2,3,4
:

\displaystyle g_{f,2,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{l^{0}_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{1}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{1,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})-\frac{l^{0}_{1}}{l^{0}_{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{2}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{l^{0}_{1}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{1}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{1,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})+O_{p}(T^{-1/4})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{1}{l_{1}^{0}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l_{1}^{0}-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}(\mathbf{I}_{r}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}_{\mathbf{w}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}})\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}+O_{p}(T^{-1/4})

\displaystyle\to_{p}\sqrt{1-\pi_{0}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}(\mathbf{I}_{r}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}_{\mathbf{w}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}})\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0},

(2.47)

because, similarly to (
2.41
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{2}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\frac{2}{\sqrt{T}\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{2}-1}\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\delta})^{\prime}\mathbf{z}_{t}u_{t+1}\right|

\displaystyle+\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{2}-1}\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\delta})^{\prime}\mathbf{z}_{t}\mathbf{z}_{t}^{\prime}\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\delta})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\frac{2}{\sqrt{T}\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{2}-1}\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\delta})^{\prime}\mathbf{z}_{t}u_{t+1}\right|

\displaystyle+\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{2}-1}\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\delta})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{z}}\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\delta})\right|

\displaystyle+\left|\frac{1}{T}\underbrace{\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{2}-1}\left[\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\delta})\otimes\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\delta})\right]^{\prime}n^{-1/2}\mathrm{vec}\left(\mathbf{z}_{t}\mathbf{z}_{t}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{z}}\right)}_{O_{p}(1)}\right|

\displaystyle=o_{p}(1).

(2.48)

Then, by inserting (
2.33
), we have

\displaystyle g_{f,3,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{1}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}\frac{n}{l_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l_{1}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{1,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})-\frac{l_{1}}{l^{0}_{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{2}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{1}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}\frac{n}{l_{1}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l_{1}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{1,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})-\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{1}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}\frac{n}{l^{0}_{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{2}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{1}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}\frac{n}{l_{1}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l_{1}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{1,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})+O_{p}(T^{-1/4})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{1}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}\frac{1}{l_{1}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l_{1}-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}(\mathbf{I}_{r}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}_{\mathbf{w}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}})\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}+O_{p}(T^{-1/4})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{1}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}(\mathbf{I}_{r}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}_{\mathbf{w}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}})\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}+O_{p}(T^{-1/4})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{n-\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor}{n}\frac{1}{(1-\tau_{0})}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}(\mathbf{I}_{r}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}_{\mathbf{w}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}})\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}+O_{p}(T^{-1/4})

\displaystyle\to_{p}\sqrt{1-\pi_{0}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}(\mathbf{I}_{r}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}_{\mathbf{w}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}})\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0},

(2.49)

because

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{1}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}\frac{n}{l^{0}_{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{2}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n-\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\frac{n}{l_{2}^{0}}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{2}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})\right|

\displaystyle=O_{p}(T^{-1/4})

(2.50)

and by the same argument

\displaystyle g_{f,4,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{2}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}\frac{n}{l_{1}^{0}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{1}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{1,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})-\frac{l^{0}_{1}}{l_{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l_{2}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{2}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}\frac{n}{l_{1}^{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l^{0}_{1}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{1,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})-\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{2}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}\frac{n}{l_{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l_{2}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{2}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}\frac{1}{l_{1}^{0}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l_{1}^{0}-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}(\mathbf{I}_{r}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}_{\mathbf{w}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}})\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}+O_{p}(T^{-1/4})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{2}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}(\mathbf{I}_{r}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}_{\mathbf{w}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}})\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}+O_{p}(T^{-1/4})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{n}{T}}\frac{n-\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor}{n}\frac{1}{(1-\tau_{0})}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}(\mathbf{I}_{r}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}_{\mathbf{w}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}})\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}+O_{p}(T^{-1/4})

\displaystyle\to_{p}\sqrt{1-\pi_{0}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0\prime}(\mathbf{I}_{r}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}_{\mathbf{w}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{w}\mathbf{f}})\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}

(2.51)

as
T\to\infty
, since

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{2}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}\frac{n}{l_{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l_{2}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1\leq l_{2}\leq n}\left(\frac{n}{l_{2}}\right)\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{2}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l_{2}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})\right|=O_{p}(T^{-1/4}),

(2.52)

as long as
\tau_{0}\neq 0
and it is sufficiently far from it. The latter requirement is placed to make the supremum bounded. Alternatively, we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{n(1-\tau_{0})}\sum_{l_{2}=\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1}^{n}\frac{n}{l_{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l_{2}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1\leq l_{2}\leq n}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l_{2}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})\right|\frac{1}{(1-\tau_{0})}\int_{\lambda_{2}=\tau_{0}}^{1}\frac{1}{\lambda_{2}}d\lambda_{2}+o_{p}(T^{-1/4})

\displaystyle=-\sup_{\lfloor n\tau_{0}\rfloor+1\leq l_{2}\leq n}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+l_{2}-1}(\widetilde{u}^{2}_{2,t+1}-u_{t+1}^{2})\right|\frac{1}{(1-\tau_{0})}\ln(\tau_{0})+o_{p}(T^{-1/4})

\displaystyle=O_{p}(T^{-1/4}).

(2.53)

More generally, they both follow directly based on the logic in
Pitarakis, (2025)
, and in particular, the passage from (A.6) to (A.8), which demonstrates that the remainder still vanishes inside of the integral.

Appendix 3
Theoretical Results I: PCA

3.1
Relevant Expansions

3.1.1
Homogeneous
\alpha

Recall that since
\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}
is the matrix of
r
eigenvectors corresponding to the
r
largest eigenvalues of
(Nt)^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{t}\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\prime}
, we have
18
18

18

Let us note that the SVD (and the subsequent decomposition) is here conducted on the
t\times t
covariance for
\mathbf{X}^{\prime}
i.e.,
\frac{1}{Nt}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{\prime}
, following the work of
Bai & Ng, (2023)
. The same decomposition and asymptotic expansions below can be done in the (more traditional) case of taking eigenvectors of the
N\times N
covariance
\frac{1}{Nt}\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\mathbf{X}

(
Stock & Watson, 2002a,
;
Barigozzi et al.,, 2024
, see)
. Both SVDs return the same set of singular values, of course, and as long as the interest is in modeling static principal eigenvectors, the difference boils down to loadings and factors being exchanged in the various expressions.

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{2}=\frac{1}{Nt}\mathbf{X}_{t}\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{Nt}\mathbf{F}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{Nt}\mathbf{F}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{Nt}\mathbf{E}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{Nt}\mathbf{E}_{t}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\prime}\right)\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Nt}\mathbf{F}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}+\frac{1}{Nt}\mathbf{F}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}+\frac{1}{Nt}\mathbf{E}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}+\frac{1}{Nt}\mathbf{E}_{t}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t},

(3.1)

which implies that by letting the rotation matrix
\mathbf{H}_{Nt,r}=\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{-2}(t^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}^{\prime}\mathbf{F}_{t})(N^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Lambda})

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}-\mathbf{F}_{t}\mathbf{H}_{Nt,r}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}-\mathbf{F}_{t}(N^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Lambda})(t^{-1}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t})\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{-2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{Nt}\mathbf{F}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}+\frac{1}{Nt}\mathbf{E}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}+\frac{1}{Nt}\mathbf{E}_{t}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\right)\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{-2}.

(3.2)

This is the same expansion as in
Bai & Ng, (2023)
. It is possible to obtain the uniform rate of
\frac{1}{t}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}-\mathbf{F}_{t}\mathbf{H}_{Nt,r}^{\prime}\right\|^{2}=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{s=1}^{t}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{f}}_{s}-\mathbf{H}_{Nt,r}\mathbf{f}_{s}\right\|^{2}
(Frobenius norm) by applying high-level conditions, especially on the divergence rate of the spectral norm of
\mathbf{E}_{t}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\prime}
. However, while the asymptotic behavior of the whole stack is important, we are more interested in the behavior of its
s
th row for
s=1,\ldots,t
, because we will need to understand the asymptotic behavior of their
out-of-sample average
, where the rotation matrix will change for every
t=k_{0},\ldots,T-1
. This will result in a generalization of Theorem 4.1 of
Gonçalves et al., (2017)
to potentially weaker loadings. For this, we obtain an expansion similar to (
3.2
) by implementing further modifications from both
Bai & Ng, (2023)
and
Gonçalves et al., (2017)
. In particular,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}-\mathbf{F}_{t}\mathbf{H}_{Nt,r}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{N^{\alpha}t}\mathbf{F}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}+\frac{1}{N^{\alpha}t}\mathbf{E}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}+\frac{1}{N^{\alpha}t}\mathbf{E}_{t}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\right)\left(\frac{N}{N^{\alpha}}\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{2}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{N^{\alpha}t}\mathbf{F}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}+\frac{1}{N^{\alpha}t}\mathbf{E}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}+\frac{1}{N^{\alpha}t}\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{E}_{t}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\prime})\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}+\frac{1}{N^{\alpha}t}(\mathbf{E}_{t}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\prime}-\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{E}_{t}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\prime}))\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\right)\left(\frac{N}{N^{\alpha}}\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{2}\right)^{-1},

(3.3)

which will not require imposing any rates of
\mathbf{E}_{t}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\prime}
directly, but will shift focus to the covariance analysis of the panel idiosyncratic components, which will be needed in the analysis of rows. Also,
\alpha\in(0,1]
is a device which accommodates potentially weaker factor loadings, where e.g.
\alpha=1
gives the usual strong loading case, and
\alpha=0
leads to absolutely uninformative loadings. For instance, let
r=1
, then if
\frac{1}{N^{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{i}^{2}<\infty
, we have square-summable loadings, which implies that individual loadings are practically zero for highly indexed individuals. Accommodation of weaker loadings will require specific assumptions, one of them being
\frac{1}{N^{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
having a positive definite limit. There requirements are reflected in Assumption 3 iii). Note that now
\mathbf{H}_{Nt,r}=\left(\frac{N}{N^{\alpha}}\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{2}\right)^{-1}(t^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}^{\prime}\mathbf{F}_{t})(N^{-\alpha}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Lambda})
. Further we define the following scalar quantities:

\displaystyle\gamma_{l,s,\alpha}=\frac{1}{N^{\alpha}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}(e_{i,l}e_{i,s})=\frac{N}{N^{\alpha}}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}(e_{i,l}e_{i,s})=\frac{N}{N^{\alpha}}\gamma_{l,s},

(3.4)

\displaystyle\xi_{l,s,\alpha}=\frac{N}{N^{\alpha}}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(e_{i,l}e_{i,s}-\mathbb{E}(e_{i,l}e_{i,s}))=\frac{N}{N^{\alpha}}\xi_{l,s},

(3.5)

\displaystyle\eta_{l,s,\alpha}=\frac{1}{N^{\alpha}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{f}_{l}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}e_{i,s},

(3.6)

\displaystyle\nu_{l,s,\alpha}=\frac{1}{N^{\alpha}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{f}_{s}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}e_{i,l}.

(3.7)

Note that only the terms that depend on
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}
are directly scaled by
N^{-\alpha}
. Because (
3.4
) and (
3.5
) are functions of idiosyncratics only,
N/N^{\alpha}
can be seen as a ”penalty” term on the overall rate, because we estimate potentially weaker loadings with the usual PC method. Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{f}}_{s}-\mathbf{H}_{Nt,r}\mathbf{f}_{s}=\left(\frac{N}{N^{\alpha}}\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{2}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{t}\sum_{l=1}^{t}\widehat{\mathbf{f}}_{l}\gamma_{l,s,\alpha}+\frac{1}{t}\sum_{l=1}^{t}\widehat{\mathbf{f}}_{l}\xi_{l,s,\alpha}+\frac{1}{t}\sum_{l=1}^{t}\widehat{\mathbf{f}}_{l}\eta_{l,s,\alpha}+\frac{1}{t}\sum_{l=1}^{t}\widehat{\mathbf{f}}_{l}\nu_{l,s,\alpha}\right).

(3.8)

3.1.2
Heterogeneous
\alpha

Note that above imposes that the loadings are weaker to the same degree. Alternatively, we can allow for
\mathbf{B}_{N}=\mathrm{diag}(N^{\alpha_{1}/2},\ldots,N^{\alpha_{r}/2})
, where
1\geq\alpha_{1}>\alpha_{2}>\ldots>\alpha_{r}>0
(the weakest loading still cannot be absolutely uninformative). Note that
\|\mathbf{B}_{N}\|\leq MN^{\alpha_{1}/2}
and
\|\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}\|\leq mN^{-\alpha_{r}/2}
for some positive constants
M
and
m
, which means that the order of (the inverse of) this normalization matrix is dominated by the (weakest) strongest factor loading. Again, recall that
\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{2}=\frac{1}{Nt}\mathbf{X}_{t}\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}
by the eigenvalue-eigenvector relationship. Then, by using the fact that both
\mathbf{B}_{N}
and
\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{2}
are diagonal, we obtain

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{2}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}=\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{2}=\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\mathbf{B}_{N}(\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-2}\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{2})=\frac{1}{Nt}\mathbf{X}_{t}\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}.

(3.9)

By inserting the expression for
\mathbf{X}_{t}
, multiplying both sides by
N
and multiplying
\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}
in, we get

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{F}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}t^{-1}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}+\mathbf{F}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}t^{-1}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}+t^{-1}\mathbf{E}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}+t^{-1}\mathbf{E}_{t}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\right)\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}=\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\mathbf{B}_{N}(N\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-2}\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{2}),

(3.10)

which can further be turned into

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\mathbf{B}_{N}(N\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-2}\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{2})-\mathbf{F}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}t^{-1}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}=\mathbf{F}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}t^{-1}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}+t^{-1}\mathbf{E}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\mathbf{F}_{t}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}+t^{-1}\mathbf{E}_{t}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}.

(3.11)

By premultiplying both sides by
(N\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-2}\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{2})^{-1}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\mathbf{B}_{N}

\displaystyle-\mathbf{F}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}t^{-1}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}(N\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-2}\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{2})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\mathbf{B}_{N}-\mathbf{F}_{t}\mathbf{B}_{N}\left[\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}t^{-1}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}(N\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-2}\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{2})^{-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{F}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}t^{-1}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}+t^{-1}\mathbf{E}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}+t^{-1}\mathbf{E}_{t}\mathbf{E}_{t}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}\right)(N\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-2}\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{2})^{-1},

(3.12)

where we define
\overline{\mathbf{H}}_{Nt,r}=(N\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-2}\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{2})^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}t^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}^{\prime}\mathbf{F}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}
. The component
\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}t^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}^{\prime}\mathbf{F}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}
is bounded in probability for
t=T
as argued in
Bai & Ng, (2023)
, and we will show that it is bounded uniformly in the upcoming Lemma 1. At this stage, note that by using the fact that the product of
\mathbf{B}_{N}
and
\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{2}
commutes, we have the property

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}\overline{\mathbf{H}}_{Nt,r}\mathbf{B}_{N}=\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}N^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{2}\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{-2}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}t^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}^{\prime}\mathbf{F}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}N^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{2}\mathbf{B}_{N}^{-1}\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{-2}t^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}^{\prime}\mathbf{F}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{D}_{Nt,r}^{-2}(t^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}^{\prime}\mathbf{F}_{t})(N^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}),

(3.13)

which is the original rotation defined below (
3.1
). Therefore, the operations performed in (
3.10
) - (
